  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, my regular doctor sent me here. She says I've gotten too thin. I mean, I guess I can see it a little bit—my work pants are definitely hanging pretty loose on me lately. I work as a janitor over at the community college, you know, the one off Route 9? It's a big campus. I'm on my feet all night, emptying trash, buffing the floors in the science building. They just put in these new linoleum tiles in the labs, and let me tell you, they scuff if you even look at them wrong. I have to use a special cleaner now that smells like awful fake pine... But anyway, about the weight, it's just... part of me still feels relieved when I step on the scale and the number is lower. It's hard to explain.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Oh, gosh, yes. I do. Like, if I eat something heavier, you know, like biscuits or pasta... Actually, they serve this baked ziti in the student dining hall on Tuesdays. The smell just drifts all the way down the corridor while I'm trying to mop the baseboards. I actually took a few courses there years ago—mostly art history, just for myself, before I needed the health insurance from the custodial job. The cafeteria food was terrible back then, all mystery meat and soggy peas, but now they have these fancy stations with made-to-order everything. \n\nBut anyway, what was I... right, if I do eat something heavy like that pasta, I spend the rest of the day just terrified it's going to show on me right away. I'll keep checking my reflection in the glass doors of the library when I walk by, just thinking about how I need to make up for it somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens no. I could never do that. I actually hate throwing up. You know, working as a janitor, I see my fair share of... well, messes in the restrooms. Especially on Sunday mornings after the college kids have been out partying all weekend. The dorm bathrooms are just awful. I cover for Margaret sometimes in the east wing dorms—she's been having terrible knee trouble lately. Poor thing needs a replacement, but the health plan we're on won't cover the newer ceramic joint, only the standard metal one. Can you believe that? After thirty years of service to the school! But anyway, cleaning up after those sick kids really cures you of ever wanting to throw up yourself. So no, I never do anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise to try to burn off calories after eating?\n\nPatient: Exercise? No, not really. I mean, I don't go to a gym or run on a treadmill or anything like that. Honestly, I don't have the energy for it most days anyway. Between the night shifts and... well, just being on my feet. My shoes are practically worn through. I buy those orthopedic ones, you know, the ugly white ones that nurses wear? They're expensive, but if you don't have good arch support, your back will just scream at you. I learned that the hard way my first year on the job. I used to wear these little canvas sneakers, and my sciatica flared up so badly I couldn't even bend over to pick up a dropped pencil. My supervisor, Tom, he's a stickler for uniform, but even he took one look at me hobbling around and told me to get proper shoes. He's retiring next month, actually. Moving down to Florida to live near his daughter. I don't know who they're going to replace him with, but I hope it's not that new guy from maintenance. He thinks he knows everything about floor wax, but he doesn't know a thing. But no, I don't do any extra exercising. My job is enough of a workout.\n\nDoctor: Do you limit what you eat or avoid certain foods because you're afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I do. I mean, I cook a lot. I make these big plates for my husband and my mother-in-law. She moved in with us last spring after she had a little fall in her kitchen. Nothing broken, thank goodness, but she was so shaken up. Her house was just too big for her anyway, all those stairs. We had to convert the den into a bedroom for her, which meant moving all of my husband's model trains down to the basement. He wasn't too happy about that, let me tell you. He's been building this replica of the Santa Fe line since 1998. The detail on it is incredible, little tiny people waiting at the stations and everything. He spends hours down there with his magnifying glass and tweezers. Sometimes the smell of the glue gives me a headache, though, so I have to open the little hopper windows, even in the dead of winter. \n\nBut anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right, the food. So I'll cook these nice dinners for them, meatloaf or casseroles, but by the time I actually sit down at the table... I just, I usually tell myself that a cup of black coffee, maybe a few vegetables, or just half a piece of dry toast is really enough for me. I just can't bring myself to eat the rest of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be fat even if other people thought you were thin?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I do. It's just... when I stay small and neat, I feel like I'm handling everything right, you know? Keeping things under control. But if my stomach looks the least bit full in the mirror, I just feel like I've completely let myself go. It's like when I'm buffing the floors in the main hallway at the science building. You get that wax laid down perfectly smooth, not a single scuff mark, and it just shines like glass. It's so satisfying. But then the bell rings, and all those students come trampling out of their chemistry labs with their heavy boots, especially when it's raining or snowing out. The salt they put on the sidewalks in the winter is just a nightmare for those floors. It eats right through the finish. We go through buckets of neutralizer from November to March. And the mop heads get so disgusting, you have to soak them in bleach overnight just to get the gray out. The purchasing department tried to switch us to these cheaper synthetic mop heads last year to save money, but they just push the dirt around instead of picking it up. I had to go right up to the facilities manager and show him the difference. I brought in a clean white towel and wiped it across a floor I'd just mopped with their cheap string, and it came up black. He switched us back to the cotton ones the very next week. But yes, if I see even a little bump on my stomach, I just feel awful, no matter what anyone else says.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is lower than what other people think it is?\n\nPatient: My doctor says I've gotten too thin, actually. And I suppose... well, I can see my work pants are hanging loose on me lately. I have to wear my belt pulled to the very last notch. We get our uniforms through this supply company, and they changed the fabric last year to save money. It’s this awful polyester blend that just doesn't breathe at all. And the industrial washing machines down in the maintenance basement, they just chew up the hems. I'm constantly having to get out my sewing kit on my lunch break. \n\nMy mother taught me how to hem when I was just a little girl, back when they actually taught home economics in school. You don't see that anymore, do you? Kids today can't even sew on a button. My granddaughter, Lily, she brought me a winter jacket last Thanksgiving with a torn lining, expecting me to fix it right there while the turkey was in the oven. I love her to pieces, she's a sweet girl, studying communications, but she's twenty-two years old, she should know how to thread a needle by now. \n\nBut... what was I saying? Oh, my weight. Right. Well, even with the doctor saying I'm too thin and my pants bagging on me, I have to admit... part of me still feels relieved when I step on the scale in the morning and the number is lower. I just feel better seeing it go down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there is a specific weight or body shape that you need to achieve to feel safe or in control?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't really have a specific number in mind, if that's what you mean. I don't think there's a magic weight that makes everything perfect. It's more just... wanting it to be lower, I guess. Though speaking of numbers, my husband has this whole spreadsheet for his train layout. He keeps track of every single piece of track, every little plastic tree, and exactly what he paid for them. He prints it out at the end of every month and puts it in this big blue binder. The binder is getting so thick, the rings are starting to separate. I told him he should really back it up on a thumb drive, because what if something happens to the paper copy? We had a pipe burst in the basement back in 2015, right near the water heater. The plumber said it was a faulty valve that they don't even make anymore. It flooded the whole corner where we keep the holiday decorations. All my Christmas ornaments from when the kids were little, the handmade ones with the macaroni and the glitter, completely ruined. I was heartbroken. I tried to dry them out on newspaper in the garage, but the macaroni just turned to mush and smelled terrible. We had to throw out three whole boxes of memories. It took the restoration company a week with those giant fans to dry out the drywall. \n\nSo yes, I don't really have a certain weight I'm trying to reach. I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because you don't want to look a certain way?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose it is about how I look. If I eat something heavier, you know, like biscuits or a big bowl of pasta, I just spend the rest of the entire day terrified it's going to show on me. I'll just keep checking the mirror in the staff restroom, pulling my shirt tight to see, and thinking I need to find a way to make up for it. \n\nSpeaking of pasta, they serve the most dreadful baked ziti in the cafeteria on Thursdays. The students leave half-eaten plates of it everywhere, and the sauce gets crusted onto the tables if I don't get to it right away with the degreaser. And the smell... it lingers in the hallways until Friday morning. It reminds me of this Italian restaurant my husband and I used to go to for our anniversary, Luigi's. They had these red and white checkered tablecloths and little candles in Chianti bottles. But they closed down during the pandemic. The owner, Mr. Moretti, he retired and moved to Florida. Naples, I think. Or maybe Sarasota. He had a cousin down there who ran a citrus grove. They sent us a box of honeybell oranges one Christmas, and they were just the sweetest things. You had to eat them over the sink because they were so juicy. But anyway, yes, if I eat something heavy like that, I just panic about it showing on my stomach and feel like I have to fix it somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look fat even when others tell you that you are thin?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I do. It's funny, because when I stay small and neat, I feel like I'm handling everything right, you know? Like things are in order. But if my stomach looks the least bit full in the mirror, I just feel like I've completely let myself go. \n\nSpeaking of mirrors, the ones in the second-floor women's restroom are just impossible to keep clean. The lighting in there is so harsh, fluorescent tubes that buzz all day long. I put in a work order for the ballast three weeks ago, but maintenance is so backed up because of the new HVAC installation in the science wing. They've got contractors coming in and out all day, tracking mud everywhere. I had to mop the main corridor four times yesterday. And the mud here isn't like normal dirt, it's this awful red clay. My husband used to try and grow tomatoes in our backyard when we first bought the house, but the soil was just solid clay. He'd be out there with a pickaxe on Saturdays, sweating through his t-shirt, trying to break it up to mix in the peat moss. We eventually gave up and just built raised beds out of cedar planks. Cedar is so expensive now, though. You go to the lumber yard and a single board costs what a whole tree used to. \n\nBut yes, if I look in the mirror and see any kind of fullness, I just feel awful, like I'm fat and I've lost control of everything.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about gaining weight ever caused problems in your relationships or daily activities?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it has, a bit. I mean, I still get my cleaning shifts done at the school, I make sure of that. But I've started avoiding family dinners, and I don't go to the church potlucks anymore. My husband says I'm physically around everyone but I'm not really joining in. \n\nSpeaking of the church potlucks, Mrs. Higgins always brings this green bean casserole with those crispy fried onions on top. She buys the onions in bulk from one of those warehouse club stores. We used to have a membership to one of those places, the one out on Route 9, but we let it lapse because we just couldn't eat a five-pound bag of shredded cheddar cheese before it went moldy. My husband tried freezing it once, but when it thaws out, it gets all crumbly and the texture is just completely wrong for melting over nachos. We used to make nachos on Sunday afternoons while watching the football game. He's a huge fan of the local team, has all the jerseys and the foam fingers. They had a really bad season last year, though. The quarterback tore his ACL in the third game and they had to bring up a rookie from the practice squad. The poor kid looked completely lost out there on the field, just terrified every time the defensive line broke through. \n\nBut anyway, yes, my husband feels like I'm just sitting there at the table without actually being a part of things, since I won't eat with them.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't really know. I mean, my husband says I'm wasting away, but when I look at myself, I just see... I see where things are soft, where they shouldn't be. \n\nSpeaking of soft, the paper towels they just ordered for the school dispensers are absolutely terrible. The district switched suppliers to save a few pennies, and now these new ones just dissolve the second they get wet. You try to wipe down the mirrors or the sinks, and you're just pushing wet paper pulp around. It's like that cheap toilet paper we had at the cabin we rented up by the lake a few summers ago. Oh, that cabin was beautiful, though. It had this wraparound porch with a swing, and you could sit out there in the morning with your coffee and watch the loons. There was a family of them that nested near the dock. The babies would ride on the mother's back. We'd take the canoe out—my husband insisted on paddling, even though his shoulder was bothering him—and we'd glide right past them. The water was so clear you could see the rocks at the bottom. We found a little bait shop in town that sold the most amazing homemade fudge. The woman who ran it, she'd been making it for forty years, using her grandmother's recipe. She had this old brass cash register that went *ding* when she opened the drawer. \n\nBut anyway, what were you asking? About how others see me? I'm not really sure, I just know what I see when I look at myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the main reason you feel anxious or upset?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I think it really is the main reason. It's just like I was saying a few minutes ago, you know? When I manage to stay small and neat, my anxiety is mostly quiet because I feel like I'm handling everything right. But the second I look in the mirror and feel like my stomach is full, I get terribly upset. It just feels like I've completely let myself go, like everything is a mess and I can't control it. \n\nI just can't stand messes. Speaking of which, you should see the physical education closet at the middle school right now. The new gym teacher, Mr. Peterson, he just throws the volleyballs and the dodgeballs in there wherever they land instead of putting them back in the mesh bags. It drives me absolutely crazy. I went in there on Tuesday to grab a fresh dust mop, and I tripped right over a jump rope that was just left in a tangle on the floor. I bruised my knee pretty badly on the doorframe. My husband told me I should put some of that arnica cream on it. He swears by that stuff ever since he pulled a muscle in his back shoveling snow last winter. \n\nWe had that massive blizzard, remember? Three feet of snow in two days. The city plows didn't come down our street for almost a week because we live on that dead-end cul-de-sac. We had to live off canned soup and whatever was left in the chest freezer in the basement. We actually had this huge glazed ham down there from Easter that we never finished, so we ended up eating ham sandwiches for four days straight. I used to love baking my own bread for sandwiches, but yeast has gotten so expensive at the grocery store... but anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, my anxiety. Yes, it's mostly just about my stomach and keeping things small.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the main thing that determines your self-worth?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, absolutely. It's really just what I was saying a moment ago, honestly. My whole sense of whether I'm worth anything at all comes down to that. When I keep myself small and neat, I feel valuable, like I'm handling my life right. But if I look in the mirror and my stomach looks even the slightest bit full, my self-worth just plummets. I feel like a total failure, like I've completely let myself go. \n\nIt's like letting a house go, you know? Speaking of which, there's this abandoned property at the end of our street that the bank just took over. The gutters are literally falling off the roof, and the weeds in the front yard are waist-high. It's a shame, because it used to be such a lovely split-level. The family that lived there before, the Millers, they had this gorgeous golden retriever named Buster. He used to escape their fence and come dig up my tulip bulbs. I tried putting chicken wire down, but he'd just dig right under it. Chicken wire is so flimsy these days anyway. I bought a roll at the hardware store last spring to put around my tomato plants, because the rabbits were getting to them. The rabbits around here are just fearless. One of them sat right on the patio furniture while I was drinking my morning tea, just staring at me. My husband said we should get a dog to scare them off, but with my cleaning schedule at the school, it wouldn't be fair to leave a poor animal locked up all day. Plus, pet food has gotten so ridiculously expensive, and you never know what they're putting in those kibbles anymore. I saw a documentary once about meat byproducts... \n\nBut I'm sorry, I lost my train of thought. What were you asking about? Oh, right, my worth. Yes, it really just depends entirely on keeping my stomach flat and staying small.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how it actually looks in pictures or to other people?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't really know. Like I said before, I just know what I see when I look at myself in the mirror. I mean, my husband says things, but I don't know if pictures look different or anything. I just see what I see. \n\nSpeaking of pictures, though, they just updated the staff directory photos at the middle school. They had this local photographer come in, a young guy with one of those fancy digital cameras that looks like a spaceship. He set up this whole backdrop in the library, right in front of the biography section. Which reminds me, I had to spend an hour dusting those top shelves before he got there because the librarian, Mrs. Higgins, has been out on medical leave. She had to have her gallbladder removed. It's a shame, really, because she's the only one who knows how to fix the laminating machine when it jams. The substitute they brought in tried to laminate some construction paper pumpkins for the fall bulletin board, and the whole thing just melted into this awful, sticky mess. I had to use three different kinds of solvent to get the rollers clean. \n\nSolvents are getting so weak these days, you know? They must be taking all the good chemicals out for environmental reasons. I remember back when I first started cleaning, we had this floor wax stripper that could take the finish off a battleship. You had to wear thick rubber gloves, the yellow ones that go all the way up to your elbows. My grandmother used to wear gloves like that when she did the canning in the late summer. She'd put up dozens of jars of bread and butter pickles, and the whole house would smell like vinegar and mustard seed for weeks. We'd store them in the root cellar, right next to the potatoes...\n\nBut anyway, what was the question? About pictures? Yeah, I'm not really sure about that. I just focus on keeping myself neat.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how it actually is, regardless of what others say?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't know about \"different from how it actually is.\" Like I keep saying, I just know what I see in the mirror. When I stay small and neat, I feel like I'm handling everything right, but if my stomach looks the least bit full in the mirror, I feel like I've let myself go. It's just a mess. \n\nAnd like I said, I can't stand messes. Even my own husband makes messes I have to clean up. He was trying to fix the leaky faucet in the downstairs half-bath last weekend, and he left his wrenches and plumber's tape all over the vanity. He used that white Teflon tape, you know? It gets everywhere if you aren't careful. He said he learned how to do it from a YouTube video, but he ended up stripping the threading on the pipe, so we had to call a real plumber anyway. The plumber charged us two hundred dollars just to walk through the door. Can you believe that? Two hundred dollars! We could have bought a whole new vanity for that price. We saw a beautiful oak one at the home improvement store last month when we were looking for a new ceiling fan for the living room. The old fan started making this awful clicking noise every time it spun around. Click, click, click... it drove me absolutely up the wall when I was trying to watch my evening stories. I love those British mystery shows, the ones where they solve murders in little quaint villages. The scenery is always so green and lovely. I've always wanted to visit England, maybe see the gardens at Sissinghurst... \n\nBut, oh, I'm sorry. I get so sidetracked. What did you ask me? About my weight being different? I really just know that if I don't look small and neat in the mirror, I feel like I've completely lost control of everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body shape or weight is normal when you look in the mirror, regardless of what others might say?\n\nPatient: Well, normal? I don't know what normal really means anymore. I just know what I see. Like I was saying, when I look in the mirror, if I stay small and neat, I feel like I'm handling everything right. But if my stomach looks the least bit full, I just feel like I've completely let myself go. It's just awful. \n\nIt's that same feeling I get when I walk into the boys' locker room at the school after a basketball game. The smell, the wet towels everywhere... it's just a complete lack of discipline. The coach, Mr. Henderson, he tries his best, but he's so distracted these days. His wife just had twins, a boy and a girl. They named them Liam and Olivia. Such popular names right now, aren't they? It seems like every third child is named Liam. When I was growing up, it was all Johns and Marys. My best friend in grade school was named Mary Margaret. She had this incredible collection of glass horses. We used to spend hours arranging them on her windowsill so the sunlight would catch the colored glass. She had this one cobalt blue one that I absolutely adored. I tried to find one like it at an antique store a few years ago, but antique stores aren't what they used to be. It's all just overpriced junk and old Tupperware now. My mother had a whole set of that olive green Tupperware from the seventies. The lids never fit quite right after a while, especially the big one she used for potato salad...\n\nI'm sorry, I'm doing it again, aren't I? Rambling. What did you ask me? About normal? I just know that if my stomach isn't completely flat when I look in the glass, I feel like a total mess.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Plus, pet food has gotten so ridiculously expensive, and you never know what they're putting in those kibbles anymore. I saw a documentary once about meat byproducts... \n\nBut I'm sorry, I lost my train of thought. What were you asking about? Oh, right, my worth. Yes, it really just depends entirely on keeping my stomach flat and staying small.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how it actually looks in pictures or to other people?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't really know. Like I said before, I just know what I see when I look at myself in the mirror. I mean, my husband says things, but I don't know if pictures look different or anything. I just see what I see. \n\nSpeaking of pictures, though, they just updated the staff directory photos at the middle school. They had this local photographer come in, a young guy with one of those fancy digital cameras that looks like a spaceship. He set up this whole backdrop in the library, right in front of the biography section. Which reminds me, I had to spend an hour dusting those top shelves before he got there because the librarian, Mrs. Higgins, has been out on medical leave. She had to have her gallbladder removed. It's a shame, really, because she's the only one who knows how to fix the laminating machine when it jams. The substitute they brought in tried to laminate some construction paper pumpkins for the fall bulletin board, and the whole thing just melted into this awful, sticky mess. I had to use three different kinds of solvent to get the rollers clean. \n\nSolvents are getting so weak these days, you know? They must be taking all the good chemicals out for environmental reasons. I remember back when I first started cleaning, we had this floor wax stripper that could take the finish off a battleship. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Larger than it actually is? I don't know about the "actually is" part. I feel like a broken record here, Doctor, but it's just exactly what I keep coming back to. If I keep myself small and neat, I feel like I'm doing things right, like I'm handling my life. But if I look in that mirror and my stomach looks even the tiniest bit full, it just means I've completely let myself go. It's just a disaster. 

Speaking of mirrors, the ones in the girls' restrooms at the school are just a nightmare to keep clean these days. The girls press their faces right up against the glass to put on that sticky lip gloss, and they leave these greasy smudges everywhere. The standard glass cleaner doesn't even cut through it anymore. You really need vinegar. My mother always swore by white vinegar and crumpled up newspaper to clean the windows at our house. She’d save up the Sunday paper all week just for that. The funny thing is, the black ink would get all over her hands, so she'd have to scrub them with Borax at the kitchen sink afterward. Borax is wonderful stuff, you know. They used to have that television show with the 20 Mule Team... Ronald Reagan hosted it, I think. Or was it Death Valley Days? My husband loves those old westerns. He falls asleep in his leather recliner watching them every single night, and then complains the next morning that his neck hurts. I keep telling him to go to bed, but he says he's just resting his eyes... 

Anyway, I'm sorry, I lost my train of thought again. Did that answer your question about my stomach? I just know what I see when I look at it.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, my regular doctor sent me here. She says I've gotten too thin. I mean, I guess I can see it a little bit—my work pants are definitely hanging pretty loose on me lately. I work as a janitor over at the community college, you know, the one off Route 9? It's a big campus. I'm on my feet all night, emptying trash, buffing the floors in the science building. They just put in these new linoleum tiles in the labs, and let me tell you, they scuff if you even look at them wrong. I have to use a special cleaner now that smells like awful fake pine... But anyway, about the weight, it's just... part of me still feels relieved when I step on the scale and the number is lower. It's hard to explain.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Oh, gosh, yes. I do. Like, if I eat something heavier, you know, like biscuits or pasta... Actually, they serve this baked ziti in the student dining hall on Tuesdays. The smell just drifts all the way down the corridor while I'm trying to mop the baseboards. I actually took a few courses there years ago—mostly art history, just for myself, before I needed the health insurance from the custodial job. The cafeteria food was terrible back then, all mystery meat and soggy peas, but now they have these fancy stations with made-to-order everything. \n\nBut anyway, what was I... right, if I do eat something heavy like that pasta, I spend the rest of the day just terrified it's going to show on me right away. I'll keep checking my reflection in the glass doors of the library when I walk by, just thinking about how I need to make up for it somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens no. I could never do that. I actually hate throwing up. You know, working as a janitor, I see my fair share of... well, messes in the restrooms. Especially on Sunday mornings after the college kids have been out partying all weekend. The dorm bathrooms are just awful. I cover for Margaret sometimes in the east wing dorms—she's been having terrible knee trouble lately. Poor thing needs a replacement, but the health plan we're on won't cover the newer ceramic joint, only the standard metal one. Can you believe that? After thirty years of service to the school! But anyway, cleaning up after those sick kids really cures you of ever wanting to throw up yourself. So no, I never do anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise to try to burn off calories after eating?\n\nPatient: Exercise? No, not really. I mean, I don't go to a gym or run on a treadmill or anything like that. Honestly, I don't have the energy for it most days anyway. Between the night shifts and... well, just being on my feet. My shoes are practically worn through. I buy those orthopedic ones, you know, the ugly white ones that nurses wear? They're expensive, but if you don't have good arch support, your back will just scream at you. I learned that the hard way my first year on the job. I used to wear these little canvas sneakers, and my sciatica flared up so badly I couldn't even bend over to pick up a dropped pencil. My supervisor, Tom, he's a stickler for uniform, but even he took one look at me hobbling around and told me to get proper shoes. He's retiring next month, actually. Moving down to Florida to live near his daughter. I don't know who they're going to replace him with, but I hope it's not that new guy from maintenance. He thinks he knows everything about floor wax, but he doesn't know a thing. But no, I don't do any extra exercising. My job is enough of a workout.\n\nDoctor: Do you limit what you eat or avoid certain foods because you're afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I do. I mean, I cook a lot. I make these big plates for my husband and my mother-in-law. She moved in with us last spring after she had a little fall in her kitchen. Nothing broken, thank goodness, but she was so shaken up. Her house was just too big for her anyway, all those stairs. We had to convert the den into a bedroom for her, which meant moving all of my husband's model trains down to the basement. He wasn't too happy about that, let me tell you. He's been building this replica of the Santa Fe line since 1998. The detail on it is incredible, little tiny people waiting at the stations and everything. He spends hours down there with his magnifying glass and tweezers. Sometimes the smell of the glue gives me a headache, though, so I have to open the little hopper windows, even in the dead of winter. \n\nBut anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right, the food. So I'll cook these nice dinners for them, meatloaf or casseroles, but by the time I actually sit down at the table... I just, I usually tell myself that a cup of black coffee, maybe a few vegetables, or just half a piece of dry toast is really enough for me. I just can't bring myself to eat the rest of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be fat even if other people thought you were thin?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I do. It's just... when I stay small and neat, I feel like I'm handling everything right, you know? Keeping things under control. But if my stomach looks the least bit full in the mirror, I just feel like I've completely let myself go. It's like when I'm buffing the floors in the main hallway at the science building. You get that wax laid down perfectly smooth, not a single scuff mark, and it just shines like glass. It's so satisfying. But then the bell rings, and all those students come trampling out of their chemistry labs with their heavy boots, especially when it's raining or snowing out. The salt they put on the sidewalks in the winter is just a nightmare for those floors. It eats right through the finish. We go through buckets of neutralizer from November to March. And the mop heads get so disgusting, you have to soak them in bleach overnight just to get the gray out. The purchasing department tried to switch us to these cheaper synthetic mop heads last year to save money, but they just push the dirt around instead of picking it up. I had to go right up to the facilities manager and show him the difference. I brought in a clean white towel and wiped it across a floor I'd just mopped with their cheap string, and it came up black. He switched us back to the cotton ones the very next week. But yes, if I see even a little bump on my stomach, I just feel awful, no matter what anyone else says.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is lower than what other people think it is?\n\nPatient: My doctor says I've gotten too thin, actually. And I suppose... well, I can see my work pants are hanging loose on me lately. I have to wear my belt pulled to the very last notch. We get our uniforms through this supply company, and they changed the fabric last year to save money. It’s this awful polyester blend that just doesn't breathe at all. And the industrial washing machines down in the maintenance basement, they just chew up the hems. I'm constantly having to get out my sewing kit on my lunch break. \n\nMy mother taught me how to hem when I was just a little girl, back when they actually taught home economics in school. You don't see that anymore, do you? Kids today can't even sew on a button. My granddaughter, Lily, she brought me a winter jacket last Thanksgiving with a torn lining, expecting me to fix it right there while the turkey was in the oven. I love her to pieces, she's a sweet girl, studying communications, but she's twenty-two years old, she should know how to thread a needle by now. \n\nBut... what was I saying? Oh, my weight. Right. Well, even with the doctor saying I'm too thin and my pants bagging on me, I have to admit... part of me still feels relieved when I step on the scale in the morning and the number is lower. I just feel better seeing it go down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there is a specific weight or body shape that you need to achieve to feel safe or in control?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't really have a specific number in mind, if that's what you mean. I don't think there's a magic weight that makes everything perfect. It's more just... wanting it to be lower, I guess. Though speaking of numbers, my husband has this whole spreadsheet for his train layout. He keeps track of every single piece of track, every little plastic tree, and exactly what he paid for them. He prints it out at the end of every month and puts it in this big blue binder. The binder is getting so thick, the rings are starting to separate. I told him he should really back it up on a thumb drive, because what if something happens to the paper copy? We had a pipe burst in the basement back in 2015, right near the water heater. The plumber said it was a faulty valve that they don't even make anymore. It flooded the whole corner where we keep the holiday decorations. All my Christmas ornaments from when the kids were little, the handmade ones with the macaroni and the glitter, completely ruined. I was heartbroken. I tried to dry them out on newspaper in the garage, but the macaroni just turned to mush and smelled terrible. We had to throw out three whole boxes of memories. It took the restoration company a week with those giant fans to dry out the drywall. \n\nSo yes, I don't really have a certain weight I'm trying to reach. I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because you don't want to look a certain way?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose it is about how I look. If I eat something heavier, you know, like biscuits or a big bowl of pasta, I just spend the rest of the entire day terrified it's going to show on me. I'll just keep checking the mirror in the staff restroom, pulling my shirt tight to see, and thinking I need to find a way to make up for it. \n\nSpeaking of pasta, they serve the most dreadful baked ziti in the cafeteria on Thursdays. The students leave half-eaten plates of it everywhere, and the sauce gets crusted onto the tables if I don't get to it right away with the degreaser. And the smell... it lingers in the hallways until Friday morning. It reminds me of this Italian restaurant my husband and I used to go to for our anniversary, Luigi's. They had these red and white checkered tablecloths and little candles in Chianti bottles. But they closed down during the pandemic. The owner, Mr. Moretti, he retired and moved to Florida. Naples, I think. Or maybe Sarasota. He had a cousin down there who ran a citrus grove. They sent us a box of honeybell oranges one Christmas, and they were just the sweetest things. You had to eat them over the sink because they were so juicy. But anyway, yes, if I eat something heavy like that, I just panic about it showing on my stomach and feel like I have to fix it somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look fat even when others tell you that you are thin?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I do. It's funny, because when I stay small and neat, I feel like I'm handling everything right, you know? Like things are in order. But if my stomach looks the least bit full in the mirror, I just feel like I've completely let myself go. \n\nSpeaking of mirrors, the ones in the second-floor women's restroom are just impossible to keep clean. The lighting in there is so harsh, fluorescent tubes that buzz all day long. I put in a work order for the ballast three weeks ago, but maintenance is so backed up because of the new HVAC installation in the science wing. They've got contractors coming in and out all day, tracking mud everywhere. I had to mop the main corridor four times yesterday. And the mud here isn't like normal dirt, it's this awful red clay. My husband used to try and grow tomatoes in our backyard when we first bought the house, but the soil was just solid clay. He'd be out there with a pickaxe on Saturdays, sweating through his t-shirt, trying to break it up to mix in the peat moss. We eventually gave up and just built raised beds out of cedar planks. Cedar is so expensive now, though. You go to the lumber yard and a single board costs what a whole tree used to. \n\nBut yes, if I look in the mirror and see any kind of fullness, I just feel awful, like I'm fat and I've lost control of everything.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about gaining weight ever caused problems in your relationships or daily activities?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it has, a bit. I mean, I still get my cleaning shifts done at the school, I make sure of that. But I've started avoiding family dinners, and I don't go to the church potlucks anymore. My husband says I'm physically around everyone but I'm not really joining in. \n\nSpeaking of the church potlucks, Mrs. Higgins always brings this green bean casserole with those crispy fried onions on top. She buys the onions in bulk from one of those warehouse club stores. We used to have a membership to one of those places, the one out on Route 9, but we let it lapse because we just couldn't eat a five-pound bag of shredded cheddar cheese before it went moldy. My husband tried freezing it once, but when it thaws out, it gets all crumbly and the texture is just completely wrong for melting over nachos. We used to make nachos on Sunday afternoons while watching the football game. He's a huge fan of the local team, has all the jerseys and the foam fingers. They had a really bad season last year, though. The quarterback tore his ACL in the third game and they had to bring up a rookie from the practice squad. The poor kid looked completely lost out there on the field, just terrified every time the defensive line broke through. \n\nBut anyway, yes, my husband feels like I'm just sitting there at the table without actually being a part of things, since I won't eat with them.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't really know. I mean, my husband says I'm wasting away, but when I look at myself, I just see... I see where things are soft, where they shouldn't be. \n\nSpeaking of soft, the paper towels they just ordered for the school dispensers are absolutely terrible. The district switched suppliers to save a few pennies, and now these new ones just dissolve the second they get wet. You try to wipe down the mirrors or the sinks, and you're just pushing wet paper pulp around. It's like that cheap toilet paper we had at the cabin we rented up by the lake a few summers ago. Oh, that cabin was beautiful, though. It had this wraparound porch with a swing, and you could sit out there in the morning with your coffee and watch the loons. There was a family of them that nested near the dock. The babies would ride on the mother's back. We'd take the canoe out—my husband insisted on paddling, even though his shoulder was bothering him—and we'd glide right past them. The water was so clear you could see the rocks at the bottom. We found a little bait shop in town that sold the most amazing homemade fudge. The woman who ran it, she'd been making it for forty years, using her grandmother's recipe. She had this old brass cash register that went *ding* when she opened the drawer. \n\nBut anyway, what were you asking? About how others see me? I'm not really sure, I just know what I see when I look at myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the main reason you feel anxious or upset?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I think it really is the main reason. It's just like I was saying a few minutes ago, you know? When I manage to stay small and neat, my anxiety is mostly quiet because I feel like I'm handling everything right. But the second I look in the mirror and feel like my stomach is full, I get terribly upset. It just feels like I've completely let myself go, like everything is a mess and I can't control it. \n\nI just can't stand messes. I went in there on Tuesday to grab a fresh dust mop, and I tripped right over a jump rope that was just left in a tangle on the floor. I bruised my knee pretty badly on the doorframe. My husband told me I should put some of that arnica cream on it. He swears by that stuff ever since he pulled a muscle in his back shoveling snow last winter. \n\nWe had that massive blizzard, remember? Three feet of snow in two days. The city plows didn't come down our street for almost a week because we live on that dead-end cul-de-sac. We had to live off canned soup and whatever was left in the chest freezer in the basement. We actually had this huge glazed ham down there from Easter that we never finished, so we ended up eating ham sandwiches for four days straight. I used to love baking my own bread for sandwiches, but yeast has gotten so expensive at the grocery store... but anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, my anxiety. Yes, it's mostly just about my stomach and keeping things small.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the main thing that determines your self-worth?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, absolutely. When I keep myself small and neat, I feel valuable, like I'm handling my life right. But if I look in the mirror and my stomach looks even the slightest bit full, my self-worth just plummets. I feel like a total failure, like I've completely let myself go. \n\nIt's like letting a house go, you know? I tried putting chicken wire down, but he'd just dig right under it. Chicken wire is so flimsy these days anyway. I bought a roll at the hardware store last spring to put around my tomato plants, because the rabbits were getting to them. The rabbits around here are just fearless. One of them sat right on the patio furniture while I was drinking my morning tea, just staring at me. My husband said we should get a dog to scare them off, but with my cleaning schedule at the school, it wouldn't be fair to leave a poor animal locked up all day. Plus, pet food has gotten so ridiculously expensive, and you never know what they're putting in those kibbles anymore. We used to spend hours arranging them on her windowsill so the sunlight would catch the colored glass. She had this one cobalt blue one that I absolutely adored. I tried to find one like it at an antique store a few years ago, but antique stores aren't what they used to be. It's all just overpriced junk and old Tupperware now. My mother had a whole set of that olive green Tupperware from the seventies. I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because you don't want to look a certain way?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose it is about how I look. If I eat something heavier, you know, like biscuits or a big bowl of pasta, I just spend the rest of the entire day terrified it's going to show on me. I'll just keep checking the mirror in the staff restroom, pulling my shirt tight to see, and thinking I need to find a way to make up for it. \n\nSpeaking of pasta, they serve the most dreadful baked ziti in the cafeteria on Thursdays. The students leave half-eaten plates of it everywhere, and the sauce gets crusted onto the tables if I don't get to it right away with the degreaser. And the smell... it lingers in the hallways until Friday morning. It reminds me of this Italian restaurant my husband and I used to go to for our anniversary, Luigi's. They had these red and white checkered tablecloths and little candles in Chianti bottles. But they closed down during the pandemic. The owner, Mr. Moretti, he retired and moved to Florida. Naples, I think. Or maybe Sarasota. He had a cousin down there who ran a citrus grove. They sent us a box of honeybell oranges one Christmas, and they were just the sweetest things. You had to eat them over the sink because they were so juicy. But anyway, yes, if I eat something heavy like that, I just panic about it showing on my stomach and feel like I have to fix it somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look fat even when others tell you that you are thin?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I do. It's funny, because when I stay small and neat, I feel like I'm handling everything right, you know? Like things are in order. But if my stomach looks the least bit full in the mirror, I just feel like I've completely let myself go. \n\nSpeaking of mirrors, the ones in the second-floor women's restroom are just impossible to keep clean. The lighting in there is so harsh, fluorescent tubes that buzz all day long. I put in a work order for the ballast three weeks ago, but maintenance is so backed up because of the new HVAC installation in the science wing. They've got contractors coming in and out all day, tracking mud everywhere. I had to mop the main corridor four times yesterday. And the mud here isn't like normal dirt, it's this awful red clay. My husband used to try and grow tomatoes in our backyard when we first bought the house, but the soil was just solid clay. He'd be out there with a pickaxe on Saturdays, sweating through his t-shirt, trying to break it up to mix in the peat moss. We eventually gave up and just built raised beds out of cedar planks. Cedar is so expensive now, though. You go to the lumber yard and a single board costs what a whole tree used to. \n\nBut yes, if I look in the mirror and see any kind of fullness, I just feel awful, like I'm fat and I've lost control of everything.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about gaining weight ever caused problems in your relationships or daily activities?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it has, a bit. I mean, I still get my cleaning shifts done at the school, I make sure of that. But I've started avoiding family dinners, and I don't go to the church potlucks anymore. My husband says I'm physically around everyone but I'm not really joining in. \n\nSpeaking of the church potlucks, Mrs. Higgins always brings this green bean casserole with those crispy fried onions on top. She buys the onions in bulk from one of those warehouse club stores. We used to have a membership to one of those places, the one out on Route 9, but we let it lapse because we just couldn't eat a five-pound bag of shredded cheddar cheese before it went moldy. My husband tried freezing it once, but when it thaws out, it gets all crumbly and the texture is just completely wrong for melting over nachos. We used to make nachos on Sunday afternoons while watching the football game. He's a huge fan of the local team, has all the jerseys and the foam fingers. They had a really bad season last year, though. The quarterback tore his ACL in the third game and they had to bring up a rookie from the practice squad. The poor kid looked completely lost out there on the field, just terrified every time the defensive line broke through. \n\nBut anyway, yes, my husband feels like I'm just sitting there at the table without actually being a part of things, since I won't eat with them.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't really know. I mean, my husband says I'm wasting away, but when I look at myself, I just see... I see where things are soft, where they shouldn't be. \n\nSpeaking of soft, the paper towels they just ordered for the school dispensers are absolutely terrible. The district switched suppliers to save a few pennies, and now these new ones just dissolve the second they get wet. You try to wipe down the mirrors or the sinks, and you're just pushing wet paper pulp around. It's like that cheap toilet paper we had at the cabin we rented up by the lake a few summers ago. Oh, that cabin was beautiful, though. It had this wraparound porch with a swing, and you could sit out there in the morning with your coffee and watch the loons. There was a family of them that nested near the dock. The babies would ride on the mother's back. We'd take the canoe out—my husband insisted on paddling, even though his shoulder was bothering him—and we'd glide right past them. The water was so clear you could see the rocks at the bottom. We found a little bait shop in town that sold the most amazing homemade fudge. The woman who ran it, she'd been making it for forty years, using her grandmother's recipe. She had this old brass cash register that went *ding* when she opened the drawer. \n\nBut anyway, what were you asking? About how others see me? I'm not really sure, I just know what I see when I look at myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the main reason you feel anxious or upset?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I think it really is the main reason. It's just like I was saying a few minutes ago, you know? When I manage to stay small and neat, my anxiety is mostly quiet because I feel like I'm handling everything right. But the second I look in the mirror and feel like my stomach is full, I get terribly upset. It just feels like I've completely let myself go, like everything is a mess and I can't control it. \n\nI just can't stand messes. Speaking of which, you should see the physical education closet at the middle school right now. The new gym teacher, Mr. Peterson, he just throws the volleyballs and the dodgeballs in there wherever they land instead of putting them back in the mesh bags. It drives me absolutely crazy. I went in there on Tuesday to grab a fresh dust mop, and I tripped right over a jump rope that was just left in a tangle on the floor. I bruised my knee pretty badly on the doorframe. My husband told me I should put some of that arnica cream on it. He swears by that stuff ever since he pulled a muscle in his back shoveling snow last winter. \n\nWe had that massive blizzard, remember? Three feet of snow in two days. The city plows didn't come down our street for almost a week because we live on that dead-end cul-de-sac. We had to live off canned soup and whatever was left in the chest freezer in the basement. We actually had this huge glazed ham down there from Easter that we never finished, so we ended up eating ham sandwiches for four days straight. I used to love baking my own bread for sandwiches, but yeast has gotten so expensive at the grocery store... but anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, my anxiety. Yes, it's mostly just about my stomach and keeping things small.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the main thing that determines your self-worth?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, absolutely. It's really just what I was saying a moment ago, honestly. My whole sense of whether I'm worth anything at all comes down to that. When I keep myself small and neat, I feel valuable, like I'm handling my life right. But if I look in the mirror and my stomach looks even the slightest bit full, my self-worth just plummets. I feel like a total failure, like I've completely let myself go. \n\nIt's like letting a house go, you know? Speaking of which, there's this abandoned property at the end of our street that the bank just took over. The gutters are literally falling off the roof, and the weeds in the front yard are waist-high. It's a shame, because it used to be such a lovely split-level. The family that lived there before, the Millers, they had this gorgeous golden retriever named Buster. He used to escape their fence and come dig up my tulip bulbs. I tried putting chicken wire down, but he'd just dig right under it. Chicken wire is so flimsy these days anyway. I bought a roll at the hardware store last spring to put around my tomato plants, because the rabbits were getting to them. The rabbits around here are just fearless. One of them sat right on the patio furniture while I was drinking my morning tea, just staring at me. My husband said we should get a dog to scare them off, but with my cleaning schedule at the school, it wouldn't be fair to leave a poor animal locked up all day. Plus, pet food has gotten so ridiculously expensive, and you never know what they're putting in those kibbles anymore. I saw a documentary once about meat byproducts... \n\nBut I'm sorry, I lost my train of thought. What were you asking about? Oh, right, my worth. Yes, it really just depends entirely on keeping my stomach flat and staying small.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how it actually looks in pictures or to other people?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't really know. Like I said before, I just know what I see when I look at myself in the mirror. I mean, my husband says things, but I don't know if pictures look different or anything. I just see what I see. \n\nSpeaking of pictures, though, they just updated the staff directory photos at the middle school. They had this local photographer come in, a young guy with one of those fancy digital cameras that looks like a spaceship. He set up this whole backdrop in the library, right in front of the biography section. Which reminds me, I had to spend an hour dusting those top shelves before he got there because the librarian, Mrs. Higgins, has been out on medical leave. She had to have her gallbladder removed. It's a shame, really, because she's the only one who knows how to fix the laminating machine when it jams. The substitute they brought in tried to laminate some construction paper pumpkins for the fall bulletin board, and the whole thing just melted into this awful, sticky mess. I had to use three different kinds of solvent to get the rollers clean. \n\nSolvents are getting so weak these days, you know? They must be taking all the good chemicals out for environmental reasons. I remember back when I first started cleaning, we had this floor wax stripper that could take the finish off a battleship. You had to wear thick rubber gloves, the yellow ones that go all the way up to your elbows. My grandmother used to wear gloves like that when she did the canning in the late summer. She'd put up dozens of jars of bread and butter pickles, and the whole house would smell like vinegar and mustard seed for weeks. We'd store them in the root cellar, right next to the potatoes...\n\nBut anyway, what was the question? About pictures? Yeah, I'm not really sure about that. I just focus on keeping myself neat.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how it actually is, regardless of what others say?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't know about \"different from how it actually is.\" Like I keep saying, I just know what I see in the mirror. When I stay small and neat, I feel like I'm handling everything right, but if my stomach looks the least bit full in the mirror, I feel like I've let myself go. It's just a mess. \n\nAnd like I said, I can't stand messes. Even my own husband makes messes I have to clean up. He was trying to fix the leaky faucet in the downstairs half-bath last weekend, and he left his wrenches and plumber's tape all over the vanity. He used that white Teflon tape, you know? It gets everywhere if you aren't careful. He said he learned how to do it from a YouTube video, but he ended up stripping the threading on the pipe, so we had to call a real plumber anyway. The plumber charged us two hundred dollars just to walk through the door. Can you believe that? Two hundred dollars! We could have bought a whole new vanity for that price. We saw a beautiful oak one at the home improvement store last month when we were looking for a new ceiling fan for the living room. The old fan started making this awful clicking noise every time it spun around. Click, click, click... it drove me absolutely up the wall when I was trying to watch my evening stories. I love those British mystery shows, the ones where they solve murders in little quaint villages. The scenery is always so green and lovely. I've always wanted to visit England, maybe see the gardens at Sissinghurst... \n\nBut, oh, I'm sorry. I get so sidetracked. What did you ask me? About my weight being different? I really just know that if I don't look small and neat in the mirror, I feel like I've completely lost control of everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body shape or weight is normal when you look in the mirror, regardless of what others might say?\n\nPatient: Well, normal? I don't know what normal really means anymore. I just know what I see. Like I was saying, when I look in the mirror, if I stay small and neat, I feel like I'm handling everything right. But if my stomach looks the least bit full, I just feel like I've completely let myself go. It's just awful. \n\nIt's that same feeling I get when I walk into the boys' locker room at the school after a basketball game. The smell, the wet towels everywhere... it's just a complete lack of discipline. The coach, Mr. Henderson, he tries his best, but he's so distracted these days. His wife just had twins, a boy and a girl. They named them Liam and Olivia. Such popular names right now, aren't they? It seems like every third child is named Liam. When I was growing up, it was all Johns and Marys. My best friend in grade school was named Mary Margaret. She had this incredible collection of glass horses. We used to spend hours arranging them on her windowsill so the sunlight would catch the colored glass. She had this one cobalt blue one that I absolutely adored. I tried to find one like it at an antique store a few years ago, but antique stores aren't what they used to be. It's all just overpriced junk and old Tupperware now. My mother had a whole set of that olive green Tupperware from the seventies. The lids never fit quite right after a while, especially the big one she used for potato salad...\n\nI'm sorry, I'm doing it again, aren't I? Rambling. What did you ask me? About normal? I just know that if my stomach isn't completely flat when I look in the glass, I feel like a total mess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your stomach appears larger to you than it actually is when you look in the mirror?\n\nPatient: Larger than it actually is? I don't know about the \"actually is\" part. I feel like a broken record here, Doctor, but it's just exactly what I keep coming back to. If I keep myself small and neat, I feel like I'm doing things right, like I'm handling my life. But if I look in that mirror and my stomach looks even the tiniest bit full, it just means I've completely let myself go. It's just a disaster. \n\nSpeaking of mirrors, the ones in the girls' restrooms at the school are just a nightmare to keep clean these days. The girls press their faces right up against the glass to put on that sticky lip gloss, and they leave these greasy smudges everywhere. The standard glass cleaner doesn't even cut through it anymore. You really need vinegar. My mother always swore by white vinegar and crumpled up newspaper to clean the windows at our house. She’d save up the Sunday paper all week just for that. The funny thing is, the black ink would get all over her hands, so she'd have to scrub them with Borax at the kitchen sink afterward. Borax is wonderful stuff, you know. They used to have that television show with the 20 Mule Team... Ronald Reagan hosted it, I think. Or was it Death Valley Days? My husband loves those old westerns. He falls asleep in his leather recliner watching them every single night, and then complains the next morning that his neck hurts. I keep telling him to go to bed, but he says he's just resting his eyes... \n\nAnyway, I'm sorry, I lost my train of thought again. Did that answer your question about my stomach? I just know what I see when I look at it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Distorted? I... I don't really know what to say to that. Distorted makes it sound like I'm crazy or seeing things that aren't there. But I just see what's in the mirror. It's like I've been saying, if I keep myself small and neat, then everything is fine. I'm handling my life. But if I look in the mirror and my stomach looks even the tiniest bit full, it just means I've completely let myself go. 

It's all about keeping things neat, you know? Like the supply closet at work. I spent three hours last Tuesday reorganizing all the cleaning solutions because the night shift just throws them in there willy-nilly. They put the floor wax next to the bleach, which is just a disaster waiting to happen. The new night guy, Kevin, he's barely twenty and he's always on his phone. He wears those little white earbuds all the time. I asked him what he listens to, and he said it was a podcast about true crime. I don't understand why anyone would want to listen to people talking about murders while they're trying to mop a cafeteria floor. It's so morbid. When I mop, I like it quiet. Just the sound of the water swishing in the bucket. We used to have these heavy cotton string mops, but the district switched to those microfiber ones last year to save money. They don't absorb the water nearly as well, and they leave streaks if you don't wring them out exactly right. My supervisor, Brenda, she swears by them, but Brenda hasn't actually cleaned a floor herself since 2015. She mostly just sits in her office ordering supplies and drinking that terrible instant coffee...

Oh, dear. I've completely lost the plot again, haven't I? I'm so sorry. Distorted, you said? I don't think my mind is playing tricks on me. I just know that if I don't look small and neat, I feel like a complete failure.

He wears those little white earbuds all the time. I asked him what he listens to, and he said it was a podcast about true crime. I don't understand why anyone would want to listen to people talking about murders while they're trying to mop a cafeteria floor. It's so morbid. When I mop, I like it quiet. Just the sound of the water swishing in the bucket. We used to have these heavy cotton string mops, but the district switched to those microfiber ones last year to save money. They don't absorb the water nearly as well, and they leave streaks if you don't wring them out exactly right. My supervisor, Brenda, she swears by them, but Brenda hasn't actually cleaned a floor herself since 2015. She mostly just sits in her office ordering supplies and drinking that terrible instant coffee...\n\nOh, dear. I've completely lost the plot again, haven't I? I'm so sorry. Distorted, you said? I don't think my mind is playing tricks on me. I just know that if I don't look small and neat, I feel like a complete failure."
  }
]
